That definitely feels like it developed later. Or, well, specifically after the injury. Because, like I was saying, since I can't play soccer like before, that energy is just completely trapped. Sitting around the house makes me feel like I’m literally crawling out of my skin. It’s this horrible, buzzing feeling right under the surface, and I just can't take it, so I end up pacing from room to room. \n\nI mean, I'll just walk from the living room to the kitchen, open the fridge, stare at the ketchup bottle for ten seconds, close it, walk down the hall, look out the bedroom window, and then walk right back to the kitchen. Just doing laps in my own house. My buddy came over the other day to hang out and he actually asked if I was waiting for a package or something because I just could not sit down on the couch with him to watch TV. I just kept pacing back and forth behind the sofa. It's like I'm a caged animal at the zoo or something. \n\nSo yeah, the childhood stuff was more just me being a loud kid who couldn't shut up or stay in his seat, but this crawling-out-of-my-skin, pacing-the-floors kind of restlessness? That's all from losing soccer, 100 percent. I just don't know what to do with my own body anymore when I'm just supposed to be relaxing at home.\n\nDoctor: When you were in high school—before the soccer injury—did you still have that same trouble focusing on homework and tests, or had things improved by that point?\n\nPatient: High school... man, high school is honestly kind of a blur when I try to think about the actual school part of it. Like I said before, I was definitely scatterbrained, but if you're asking if the homework and the tests specifically got better or worse... I really don't know. I can't even picture myself sitting at a desk doing homework back then. \n\nBecause, see, by the time I hit high school, my entire existence—and I mean literally everything I did from the second I woke up to the second I went to sleep—was just completely wrapped up in soccer. We had morning conditioning, then regular classes, then afternoon practice, and then usually film review or something after that. So my brain was just... it was 100 percent on the sport. \n\nI honestly couldn't tell you if my focus in class improved, or if I just sort of figured out how to fly under the radar, you know? Like, the teachers all knew who I was because I was on varsity, and the coaches were always checking our grades to make sure we were eligible to play. So I just did whatever the absolute bare minimum was to keep my GPA exactly where it needed to be. I'd get home at like seven or eight at night, completely dead on my feet, my mom would have this massive plate of food waiting for me—usually pasta or something heavy because of all the calories I was burning—and I'd just eat and pass out. \n\nI don't remember having those same moments of sitting there rushing through fifty math problems like I did in third grade, but I also don't remember ever actually sitting down and studying for a history test, either. I would just kind of show up, stare at the clock, and wait for the bell to ring so I could get back out to the locker room. \n\nSo... yeah, I don't really have a good answer for you on that one. I'm not sure if the trouble focusing went away, or if I just didn't care enough about the classes to even notice I wasn't focusing, if that makes sense? I was just surviving the school day until practice started.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask about middle school specifically—do you remember having trouble keeping up with assignments, or did your teachers ever send notes home about incomplete work or you not paying attention in class back then?\n\nPatient: Middle school... man, that's kind of a weird time to try and remember. Sixth, seventh, eighth grade... you know, it's funny, because elementary school is just so vivid in my mind with the whole desk-moving thing, and then high school, like I was just saying, was just pure, one-hundred-percent soccer. But middle school is kind of this weird, blurry gray area in the middle. \n\nI mean, that's the age when everything changes, right? You're not just in one room all day with one teacher who watches your every move. Suddenly you have, like, six different teachers for different subjects, and you have to navigate the crowded hallways, and you have to remember your locker combination—which, by the way, I was absolutely terrible at. I was always that kid yanking on the metal handle and having to go find the janitor to cut the lock or look up my combination because I just couldn't keep the numbers in my head. \n\nBut as for the actual assignments, or teachers sending notes home about me not paying attention... I honestly couldn't tell you. I just don't have a clear memory of it one way or the other. I mean, I probably was missing things? But I don't remember my mom getting those little physical notes like she did back in third grade. I think maybe by middle school, teachers just don't really send notes home the same way, you know? They expect you to be more independent. \n\nOr maybe... well, I remember they made us buy those planner things. Do you remember those? The school would hand out these little spiral-bound agendas with the school mascot on the front, and they'd tell you to write all your homework down every single day. I would always start off the year with the absolute best intentions. Like, the first week of sixth grade, I'd be writing everything down, maybe even using different colored pens for math and science, really trying to stay on top of it. But then by, like, the second week of October, that planner is just completely buried at the bottom of my locker underneath a bunch of gym shirts and crushed up Doritos bags. \n\nBut yeah, specifically remembering if I was falling behind on assignments or getting called out in class back then... I really just draw a blank. It's like I'm a caged animal at the zoo or something. \n\nSo yeah, the childhood stuff was more just me being a loud kid who couldn't shut up or stay in his seat, but this crawling-out-of-my-skin, pacing-the-floors kind of restlessness? That's all from losing soccer, 100 percent. I just don't know what to do with my own body anymore when I'm just supposed to be relaxing at home.\n\nDoctor: When you were in high school—before the soccer injury—did you still have that same trouble focusing on homework and tests, or had things improved by that point?\n\nPatient: High school... man, high school is honestly kind of a blur when I try to think about the actual school part of it. Like I said before, I was definitely scatterbrained, but if you're asking if the homework and the tests specifically got better or worse... I really don't know. I can't even picture myself sitting at a desk doing homework back then. \n\nBecause, see, by the time I hit high school, my entire existence—and I mean literally everything I did from the second I woke up to the second I went to sleep—was just completely wrapped up in soccer. We had morning conditioning, then regular classes, then afternoon practice, and then usually film review or something after that. So my brain was just... it was 100 percent on the sport. \n\nI honestly couldn't tell you if my focus in class improved, or if I just sort of figured out how to fly under the radar, you know? Like, the teachers all knew who I was because I was on varsity, and the coaches were always checking our grades to make sure we were eligible to play. So I just did whatever the absolute bare minimum was to keep my GPA exactly where it needed to be. The school would hand out these little spiral-bound agendas with the school mascot on the front, and they'd tell you to write all your homework down every single day. I would always start off the year with the absolute best intentions. Like, the first week of sixth grade, I'd be writing everything down, maybe even using different colored pens for math and science, really trying to stay on top of it. But then by, like, the second week of October, that planner is just completely buried at the bottom of my locker underneath a bunch of gym shirts and crushed up Doritos bags. \n\nBut yeah, specifically remembering if I was falling behind on assignments or getting called out in class back then... I really just draw a blank. I know you're trying to piece together that timeline, and I really wish I could give you a better picture, but... I just draw a complete blank on that specific stuff. Like, I genuinely don't remember getting in trouble for talking out of turn or anything like that once I got older. I mean, maybe I did? But if it happened, it definitely didn't stick in my brain the way the elementary school stuff did. 

You have to remember, by the time you're in high school, the whole dynamic is just completely different. In third grade, you've got one teacher who is basically hovering over you all day, watching every single thing you do. But in high school? You're just one of, like, thirty kids in a room, and honestly, half the teachers looked like they were just trying to survive until the bell rang, too. I would usually just find a desk way in the back row—ideally near the window or the radiator, because I was always freezing after our morning conditioning sessions—and I would just try to be invisible. I wasn't really the loud, disruptive kid anymore because I was just so physically exhausted all the time. When you're running sprints at six in the morning, you don't really have the energy to be talking out of turn in second-period history, you know? I was practically sleeping with my eyes open.

And as for the careless mistakes on tests or forgetting assignments... I mean, I don't have any specific memories of a teacher pulling me aside to lecture me about it. They didn't really do that anymore. If I messed up on a test, they'd just hand it back with a red C-minus scribbled on the top and move on to the next kid. And with the missing assignments, it was all just a hustle to stay eligible for the team. If a teacher warned me I was missing something, I'd just scramble and copy a worksheet from one of my teammates in the cafeteria right before class. It was never this big sit-down intervention. 

So, yeah, I just... I don't remember any of that happening in middle or high school. I wasn't getting called out, I wasn't getting in trouble... or at least, if I was, I have absolutely zero memory of it. It was all just about doing the bare minimum to get through the hallway and get back out on the field.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah, thanks for seeing me. Um, honestly, I'm just... I'm really struggling right now to get anything done. Since I'm not working right now, I've been trying to focus on, you know, getting my life together, filling out job applications and stuff. Which is already tough enough since I just have my high school diploma, so I really need to put the effort in, but... well, the main thing is I just get pulled completely off track by every little sound or thought. \n\nIt's actually ridiculous. I'll sit down at my laptop, right? I'll be trying to work on an application, maybe I'm typing in my past experience or trying to answer one of those questionnaire things, and then a car will drive by outside—just a normal car, nothing special, maybe the muffler is a bit loud—and suddenly I'm looking out the window, wondering where they're going or thinking about traffic. Or my phone screen will light up with a notification, not even a text, just like a spam email or a weather alert, and I just have to pick it up. \n\nOr even worse, it's not even something around me, just some random idea pops into my head. Like I'll suddenly wonder if I need to buy more paper towels, or I'll remember a movie I saw five years ago, and it just sends me somewhere else entirely. And the crazy part is, I don't even notice it happening. Like, an hour will go by, and I'm deep into some internet rabbit hole about, I don't know, how they build suspension bridges, and the job application is just sitting there completely blank. It's just so frustrating because I really *want* to finish it, I sit down with every intention of finishing it, but my brain just won't stay put, you know? It's like every little thing completely hijacks my attention and I just can't filter it out.\n\nDoctor: When you get distracted by these things—like the car noise or the random thoughts—are you usually worried or anxious about something, or does your mind just jump to them without any particular worry attached?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's definitely not a worry thing. I mean, don't get me wrong, being unemployed is stressful, obviously, and I do stress about that in general, but in that exact moment when the car drives by? I'm not sitting there panicking about my life or feeling anxious or anything like that. It's literally just... zip, my brain is over there now. \n\nLike, there's zero anxiety attached to the actual distraction. Take the paper towel thing I just mentioned. When I think about the paper towels, I'm not sitting there like, \"Oh my god, what if I spill something and I don't have anything to wipe it up with, what a complete disaster!\" It's nothing like that. It's more just a completely neutral, random observation that suddenly feels like the absolute most important thing in the world to figure out right that second. Like my brain just changes the channel without asking me and suddenly I'm watching a completely different show. \n\nI could be totally relaxed, you know, maybe drinking a cup of coffee, feeling pretty good about the morning, not a worry in the world, and then I'll see, I don't know, a weird bug on the windowsill. And suddenly I'm just looking at the bug. I'm not worried about the bug biting me, I'm not scared of it, I'm just looking at its legs, wondering how it climbs the glass, trying to remember what kind of bugs live in this area. So yeah, to answer your question, it's not anxiety. It's just... my attention gets completely hijacked by whatever shiny new thing enters my orbit, whether it's a sound or a thought or whatever, completely out of nowhere, and worry has nothing to do with it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with concentration and distractibility? Did this start when you were younger, or is this something that's more recent?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing. I mean, I guess I've always been a little scatterbrained, you know? Like in high school, I wasn't exactly the star pupil sitting in the front row taking perfect notes. But it was never... it was never *this* bad. This whole level of just complete inability to stay on track, where it's actually ruining my day and keeping me from getting a job? That really kicked into high gear about, I'd say, a year and ten months ago. \n\nYeah, almost exactly a year and ten months, because that's when I had this really bad soccer injury. I used to play in this adult rec league, right? Just a casual thing, but we played hard, and I ended up completely tearing my ACL. It was awful. I had to have surgery, and the recovery was just brutal. I was basically stuck on the couch for weeks, and then I had months of physical therapy. \n\nAnd before that, I had a really solid routine. I was working, I was playing soccer three nights a week, I was going to the gym on the off days... I was always moving. Always burning off energy, always having somewhere I needed to be at a specific time. But when the injury happened, all of that just stopped. Completely stopped. Suddenly I had zero structure, zero physical outlet, and it was like my brain just didn't know what to do with itself. \n\nI thought once I healed up, things would go back to normal, but they just... didn't. Even after I could walk fine again, the routine was gone, the job was gone, and this crazy distractibility just stayed. It's like the injury knocked me out of my orbit, and I've just been floating around in space ever since, unable to latch onto anything for more than five minutes. So yeah, I'd say it's been about a year and ten months of it being this disruptive.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you used to be very active—playing soccer, going to the gym, always moving around. Since your injury and losing that routine, have you noticed that you feel restless or fidgety a lot? Like, do you find yourself tapping your feet, bouncing your leg, or feeling like you need to move around, even when you're trying to sit still and focus?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Yeah, 100%. It's actually kind of funny you mention that because my buddy was just getting onto me about it the other day. Like, whenever I'm sitting down, especially if I'm sitting with someone else, my leg is just going a mile a minute. Just constantly bouncing up and down. I don't even know I'm doing it until the whole table starts shaking and whoever I'm with is like, \"Dude, chill out,\" and they have to literally put their hand on my knee to get me to stop. \n\nAnd if it's not my leg doing the bouncing, it's my hands. I literally cannot keep my hands empty. If I'm trying to sit still, I'll be clicking a pen—just click, click, click, click—driving everyone in the room crazy. Or I'll have my hand in my pocket just messing with my keys, jingling them around. I have this one keychain, it's like this metal bottle opener thing from my old soccer team, and I'll just sit there flipping it over and over in my fingers without even realizing I'm doing it. \n\nIt's exactly like you said, it's like because I'm not running up and down a field anymore, all that physical energy just got trapped inside my body and it has to leak out somehow, you know? Like a pressure valve or something. So I'm just constantly fidgeting. Even when I'm trying to do those job applications I was telling you about earlier, I'll have a pen in one hand just spinning it or clicking it while I'm trying to read the screen, which obviously doesn't help me focus at all. I just... I don't even realize it's happening. It's completely unconscious. I'll be sitting there, thinking I'm being perfectly relaxed and still, and then I look down and my leg is practically vibrating off the floor. \n\nIt's just so weird how your body does things without your brain giving the okay, right? Like, I'll be at a coffee shop—well, not so much lately since I'm trying to save money since I'm not working, but you know what I mean. If I'm anywhere where I have to just sit and be normal, the bouncing and the clicking and the key jingling just starts up completely on its own.\n\nDoctor: Going back to elementary school and middle school, before high school even—do you remember having trouble paying attention in class, or were teachers ever concerned about you being fidgety or restless back then?\n\nPatient: Oh man, elementary school. Yeah. I mean, I know I said earlier that I was scatterbrained in high school, but honestly, if I really think about it, it goes way, way back before that. \n\nLike, even back in second or third grade, I remember my teachers were just constantly—and I mean *constantly*—moving my seat around the classroom. It was almost like a running joke, except the teachers definitely weren't laughing. They'd start me out in the middle of the room, right? Sitting with a group of desks. But I just could not keep my mouth shut. If a thought popped into my head, I'd just say it out loud, right in the middle of the lesson. I'd just blurt things out, completely out of turn, or I'd be whispering to whoever was sitting next to me about some cartoon I saw or whatever. So then the teacher would sigh and move my desk to the front row. But then I'd just talk to the teacher! So then they'd move me to the very back corner, away from everyone else. \n\nAnd it wasn't just the talking, either. It was the homework. Oh man, my backpack was just this absolute black hole of crumpled up papers. I would just completely forget I even had homework. I'd get to school and everyone's turning in their folders and I'm just sitting there like, \"Wait, we had something to do?\" My mom used to get so frustrated because the teachers were always sending home these little notes about missing assignments. \n\nAnd then when we actually had to do work in class—like, you know those worksheets they'd give you? The ones with like fifty little math problems on them, or the spelling words? I would just blast through them. I just wanted to be done. I couldn't stand just sitting there working on it slowly, so I'd rush through the whole page in like two minutes flat. And then the teacher would hand it back the next day and it would just be covered in red ink. And the frustrating part was, it wasn't because I didn't know the material! It was just completely careless mistakes. Like, I'd write a plus sign instead of a minus, or I'd just completely skip an entire row of questions because my eyes jumped down the page too fast and I didn't even notice. \n\nSo yeah, they were always trying to find the \"perfect\" spot in the room to put my desk where I'd magically stop talking and actually remember my work and slow down, but it never really mattered where they put me. I was always just rushing through those worksheets and making a mess of things. I had this one teacher, Mrs. Gable in third grade, she actually taped a little square on the floor right next to her own desk and that was my spot for like half the year because I was just so all over the place... so yeah, it definitely started way back then, for sure.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to those early years—second, third grade—do you remember if you were bouncing your leg, fidgeting with things in your hands, or having that restless energy even back then, or did that physical restlessness develop later on?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because I’ve been thinking about it... back in second or third grade, I don't actually remember doing the whole leg-bouncing or the pen-clicking thing. But I think... well, I think that’s just because back then, I was always allowed to just run around, you know? You had recess, you had gym class, you rode your bike around the neighborhood until the streetlights came on. I was always just moving my whole body, so the energy never really got backed up, if that makes sense? \n\nBut this current thing, this intense physical restlessness I have right now? That definitely feels like it developed later. Or, well, specifically after the injury. Because, like I was saying, since I can't play soccer like before, that energy is just completely trapped. Sitting around the house makes me feel like I’m literally crawling out of my skin. It’s this horrible, buzzing feeling right under the surface, and I just can't take it, so I end up pacing from room to room. \n\nI mean, I'll just walk from the living room to the kitchen, open the fridge, stare at the ketchup bottle for ten seconds, close it, walk down the hall, look out the bedroom window, and then walk right back to the kitchen. Just doing laps in my own house. My buddy came over the other day to hang out and he actually asked if I was waiting for a package or something because I just could not sit down on the couch with him to watch TV. I just kept pacing back and forth behind the sofa. It's like I'm a caged animal at the zoo or something. \n\nSo yeah, the childhood stuff was more just me being a loud kid who couldn't shut up or stay in his seat, but this crawling-out-of-my-skin, pacing-the-floors kind of restlessness? That's all from losing soccer, 100 percent. I just don't know what to do with my own body anymore when I'm just supposed to be relaxing at home.\n\nDoctor: When you were in high school—before the soccer injury—did you still have that same trouble focusing on homework and tests, or had things improved by that point?\n\nPatient: High school... man, high school is honestly kind of a blur when I try to think about the actual school part of it. Like I said before, I was definitely scatterbrained, but if you're asking if the homework and the tests specifically got better or worse... I really don't know. I can't even picture myself sitting at a desk doing homework back then. \n\nBecause, see, by the time I hit high school, my entire existence—and I mean literally everything I did from the second I woke up to the second I went to sleep—was just completely wrapped up in soccer. We had morning conditioning, then regular classes, then afternoon practice, and then usually film review or something after that. So my brain was just... it was 100 percent on the sport. \n\nI honestly couldn't tell you if my focus in class improved, or if I just sort of figured out how to fly under the radar, you know? Like, the teachers all knew who I was because I was on varsity, and the coaches were always checking our grades to make sure we were eligible to play. So I just did whatever the absolute bare minimum was to keep my GPA exactly where it needed to be. I'd get home at like seven or eight at night, completely dead on my feet, my mom would have this massive plate of food waiting for me—usually pasta or something heavy because of all the calories I was burning—and I'd just eat and pass out. \n\nI don't remember having those same moments of sitting there rushing through fifty math problems like I did in third grade, but I also don't remember ever actually sitting down and studying for a history test, either. I would just kind of show up, stare at the clock, and wait for the bell to ring so I could get back out to the locker room. \n\nSo... yeah, I don't really have a good answer for you on that one. I'm not sure if the trouble focusing went away, or if I just didn't care enough about the classes to even notice I wasn't focusing, if that makes sense? I was just surviving the school day until practice started.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask about middle school specifically—do you remember having trouble keeping up with assignments, or did your teachers ever send notes home about incomplete work or you not paying attention in class back then?\n\nPatient: Middle school... man, that's kind of a weird time to try and remember. Sixth, seventh, eighth grade... you know, it's funny, because elementary school is just so vivid in my mind with the whole desk-moving thing, and then high school, like I was just saying, was just pure, one-hundred-percent soccer. But middle school is kind of this weird, blurry gray area in the middle. \n\nI mean, that's the age when everything changes, right? You're not just in one room all day with one teacher who watches your every move. Suddenly you have, like, six different teachers for different subjects, and you have to navigate the crowded hallways, and you have to remember your locker combination—which, by the way, I was absolutely terrible at. I was always that kid yanking on the metal handle and having to go find the janitor to cut the lock or look up my combination because I just couldn't keep the numbers in my head. \n\nBut as for the actual assignments, or teachers sending notes home about me not paying attention... I honestly couldn't tell you. I just don't have a clear memory of it one way or the other. I mean, I probably was missing things? But I don't remember my mom getting those little physical notes like she did back in third grade. I think maybe by middle school, teachers just don't really send notes home the same way, you know? They expect you to be more independent. \n\nOr maybe... well, I remember they made us buy those planner things. Do you remember those? The school would hand out these little spiral-bound agendas with the school mascot on the front, and they'd tell you to write all your homework down every single day. I would always start off the year with the absolute best intentions. Like, the first week of sixth grade, I'd be writing everything down, maybe even using different colored pens for math and science, really trying to stay on top of it. But then by, like, the second week of October, that planner is just completely buried at the bottom of my locker underneath a bunch of gym shirts and crushed up Doritos bags. \n\nBut yeah, specifically remembering if I was falling behind on assignments or getting called out in class back then... I really just draw a blank. It all just blends together into this awkward phase where I was just trying to figure out how the bells worked and getting really involved in club soccer outside of school. So I don't really have a specific answer for you on the middle school stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to middle school and high school, before the soccer injury—aside from just being busy with soccer—do you remember ever getting in trouble with teachers for talking out of turn, or forgetting to turn in assignments, or making careless mistakes on tests the way you did back in elementary school?\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to piece together that timeline, and I really wish I could give you a better picture, but... I just draw a complete blank on that specific stuff. Like, I genuinely don't remember getting in trouble for talking out of turn or anything like that once I got older. I mean, maybe I did? But if it happened, it definitely didn't stick in my brain the way the elementary school stuff did. \n\nYou have to remember, by the time you're in high school, the whole dynamic is just completely different. In third grade, you've got one teacher who is basically hovering over you all day, watching every single thing you do. But in high school? You're just one of, like, thirty kids in a room, and honestly, half the teachers looked like they were just trying to survive until the bell rang, too. I would usually just find a desk way in the back row—ideally near the window or the radiator, because I was always freezing after our morning conditioning sessions—and I would just try to be invisible. I wasn't really the loud, disruptive kid anymore because I was just so physically exhausted all the time. When you're running sprints at six in the morning, you don't really have the energy to be talking out of turn in second-period history, you know? I was practically sleeping with my eyes open.\n\nAnd as for the careless mistakes on tests or forgetting assignments... I mean, I don't have any specific memories of a teacher pulling me aside to lecture me about it. They didn't really do that anymore. If I messed up on a test, they'd just hand it back with a red C-minus scribbled on the top and move on to the next kid. And with the missing assignments, it was all just a hustle to stay eligible for the team. If a teacher warned me I was missing something, I'd just scramble and copy a worksheet from one of my teammates in the cafeteria right before class. It was never this big sit-down intervention. \n\nSo, yeah, I just... I don't remember any of that happening in middle or high school. I wasn't getting called out, I wasn't getting in trouble... or at least, if I was, I have absolutely zero memory of it. It was all just about doing the bare minimum to get through the hallway and get back out on the field.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like, I'd write a plus sign instead of a minus, or I'd just completely skip an entire row of questions because my eyes jumped down the page too fast and I didn't even notice. \n\nSo yeah, they were always trying to find the \"perfect\" spot in the room to put my desk where I'd magically stop talking and actually remember my work and slow down, but it never really mattered where they put me. I was always just rushing through those worksheets and making a mess of things. I had this one teacher, Mrs. Gable in third grade, she actually taped a little square on the floor right next to her own desk and that was my spot for like half the year because I was just so all over the place... so yeah, it definitely started way back then, for sure.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to those early years—second, third grade—do you remember if you were bouncing your leg, fidgeting with things in your hands, or having that restless energy even back then, or did that physical restlessness develop later on?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because I’ve been thinking about it... back in second or third grade, I don't actually remember doing the whole leg-bouncing or the pen-clicking thing. But I think... well, I think that’s just because back then, I was always allowed to just run around, you know? You had recess, you had gym class, you rode your bike around the neighborhood until the streetlights came on. I was always just moving my whole body, so the energy never really got backed up, if that makes sense? \n\nBut this current thing, this intense physical restlessness I have right now? That definitely feels like it developed later. Or, well, specifically after the injury. Because, like I was saying, since I can't play soccer like before, that energy is just completely trapped. Sitting around the house makes me feel like I’m literally crawling out of my skin. It’s this horrible, buzzing feeling right under the surface, and I just can't take it, so I end up pacing from room to room. \n\nI mean, I'll just walk from the living room to the kitchen, open the fridge, stare at the ketchup bottle for ten seconds, close it, walk down the hall, look out the bedroom window, and then walk right back to the kitchen. Just doing laps in my own house. My buddy came over the other day to hang out and he actually asked if I was waiting for a package or something because I just could not sit down on the couch with him to watch TV. I just kept pacing back and forth behind the sofa. It's like I'm a caged animal at the zoo or something. \n\nSo yeah, the childhood stuff was more just me being a loud kid who couldn't shut up or stay in his seat, but this crawling-out-of-my-skin, pacing-the-floors kind of restlessness? That's all from losing soccer, 100 percent. I just don't know what to do with my own body anymore when I'm just supposed to be relaxing at home.\n\nDoctor: When you were in high school—before the soccer injury—did you still have that same trouble focusing on homework and tests, or had things improved by that point?\n\nPatient: High school... man, high school is honestly kind of a blur when I try to think about the actual school part of it. Like I said before, I was definitely scatterbrained, but if you're asking if the homework and the tests specifically got better or worse... I really don't know. I can't even picture myself sitting at a desk doing homework back then. \n\nBecause, see, by the time I hit high school, my entire existence—and I mean literally everything I did from the second I woke up to the second I went to sleep—was just completely wrapped up in soccer. We had morning conditioning, then regular classes, then afternoon practice, and then usually film review or something after that. So my brain was just... it was 100 percent on the sport. \n\nI honestly couldn't tell you if my focus in class improved, or if I just sort of figured out how to fly under the radar, you know? Like, the teachers all knew who I was because I was on varsity, and the coaches were always checking our grades to make sure we were eligible to play. So I just did whatever the absolute bare minimum was to keep my GPA exactly where it needed to be. I'd get home at like seven or eight at night, completely dead on my feet, my mom would have this massive plate of food waiting for me—usually pasta or something heavy because of all the calories I was burning—and I'd just eat and pass out. \n\nI don't remember having those same moments of sitting there rushing through fifty math problems like I did in third grade, but I also don't remember ever actually sitting down and studying for a history test, either. I would just kind of show up, stare at the clock, and wait for the bell to ring so I could get back out to the locker room. \n\nSo... yeah, I don't really have a good answer for you on that one. I'm not sure if the trouble focusing went away, or if I just didn't care enough about the classes to even notice I wasn't focusing, if that makes sense? I was just surviving the school day until practice started.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask about middle school specifically—do you remember having trouble keeping up with assignments, or did your teachers ever send notes home about incomplete work or you not paying attention in class back then?\n\nPatient: Middle school... man, that's kind of a weird time to try and remember. Sixth, seventh, eighth grade... you know, it's funny, because elementary school is just so vivid in my mind with the whole desk-moving thing, and then high school, like I was just saying, was just pure, one-hundred-percent soccer. But middle school is kind of this weird, blurry gray area in the middle. \n\nI mean, that's the age when everything changes, right? You're not just in one room all day with one teacher who watches your every move. Suddenly you have, like, six different teachers for different subjects, and you have to navigate the crowded hallways, and you have to remember your locker combination—which, by the way, I was absolutely terrible at. I was always that kid yanking on the metal handle and having to go find the janitor to cut the lock or look up my combination because I just couldn't keep the numbers in my head. \n\nBut as for the actual assignments, or teachers sending notes home about me not paying attention... I honestly couldn't tell you. I just don't have a clear memory of it one way or the other. I mean, I probably was missing things? But I don't remember my mom getting those little physical notes like she did back in third grade. I think maybe by middle school, teachers just don't really send notes home the same way, you know? They expect you to be more independent. \n\nOr maybe... well, I remember they made us buy those planner things. Do you remember those? The school would hand out these little spiral-bound agendas with the school mascot on the front, and they'd tell you to write all your homework down every single day. I would always start off the year with the absolute best intentions. Like, the first week of sixth grade, I'd be writing everything down, maybe even using different colored pens for math and science, really trying to stay on top of it. But then by, like, the second week of October, that planner is just completely buried at the bottom of my locker underneath a bunch of gym shirts and crushed up Doritos bags. \n\nBut yeah, specifically remembering if I was falling behind on assignments or getting called out in class back then... I really just draw a blank. It all just blends together into this awkward phase where I was just trying to figure out how the bells worked and getting really involved in club soccer outside of school. So I don't really have a specific answer for you on the middle school stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to middle school and high school, before the soccer injury—aside from just being busy with soccer—do you remember ever getting in trouble with teachers for talking out of turn, or forgetting to turn in assignments, or making careless mistakes on tests the way you did back in elementary school?\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to piece together that timeline, and I really wish I could give you a better picture, but... I just draw a complete blank on that specific stuff. Like, I genuinely don't remember getting in trouble for talking out of turn or anything like that once I got older. I mean, maybe I did? But if it happened, it definitely didn't stick in my brain the way the elementary school stuff did. \n\nYou have to remember, by the time you're in high school, the whole dynamic is just completely different. In third grade, you've got one teacher who is basically hovering over you all day, watching every single thing you do. But in high school? You're just one of, like, thirty kids in a room, and honestly, half the teachers looked like they were just trying to survive until the bell rang, too. I would usually just find a desk way in the back row—ideally near the window or the radiator, because I was always freezing after our morning conditioning sessions—and I would just try to be invisible. I wasn't really the loud, disruptive kid anymore because I was just so physically exhausted all the time. When you're running sprints at six in the morning, you don't really have the energy to be talking out of turn in second-period history, you know? I was practically sleeping with my eyes open.\n\nAnd as for the careless mistakes on tests or forgetting assignments... I mean, I don't have any specific memories of a teacher pulling me aside to lecture me about it. They didn't really do that anymore. If I messed up on a test, they'd just hand it back with a red C-minus scribbled on the top and move on to the next kid. And with the missing assignments, it was all just a hustle to stay eligible for the team. If a teacher warned me I was missing something, I'd just scramble and copy a worksheet from one of my teammates in the cafeteria right before class. It was never this big sit-down intervention. \n\nSo, yeah, I just... I don't remember any of that happening in middle or high school. I wasn't getting called out, I wasn't getting in trouble... or at least, if I was, I have absolutely zero memory of it. It was all just about doing the bare minimum to get through the hallway and get back out on the field.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It was never this big sit-down intervention. \n\nSo, yeah, I just... I don't remember any of that happening in middle or high school. I wasn't getting called out, I wasn't getting in trouble... or at least, if I was, I have absolutely zero memory of it. It was all just about doing the bare minimum to get through the hallway and get back out on the field.\n\n[Last Question]\nI want to go back to high school for a second. Even though you were exhausted from soccer, do you remember struggling to focus during class or on tests back then—like, did you find your mind wandering, or did you have to re-read things multiple times because you weren't paying attention, even when you weren't tired?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / no_occupation / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sustained_Attention_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"I can start watching a repair video or reading the manual for a tool, but after a few minutes I realize I’ve been staring at it and have to back it up because none of it stuck.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Man, high school... like I said, it's just really hard to separate what was me being physically exhausted from what was just my brain not working right. I'm sure I probably did have to re-read stuff in class, but I just don't have a specific memory of sitting at a desk and doing it back then. 

But, I mean, if you're talking about that specific feeling—where your mind just wanders off and you have to go back over things—I do that constantly right now. Like, all the time. And it's not even with boring school stuff or when I'm tired, it's with things I actually *want* to be doing, when I'm wide awake. 

For example, I like to try and fix things, right? So I can start watching a repair video on YouTube, one of those step-by-step tutorials, or I'll sit down to read the manual for a new tool I just got. I'm genuinely trying to figure out how to use the thing so I don't break it. But after a few minutes, I'll suddenly sort of... snap back to reality, and I realize I've just been staring blankly at it. Like, my eyes are on the screen, the guy in the video is talking, but my brain is completely somewhere else. I haven't processed a single word for the last five minutes. My mind just completely drifted off to something else entirely—like what I'm going to eat later, or some random thought about a car I saw on the street. 

So then I have to stop, grab my phone or the mouse, and back the video up because absolutely none of it stuck. And the crazy part is, sometimes I'll rewind it, start playing it again, and then I'll zone out at the exact same spot! I'll have to rewind the same two-minute clip like three or four times just to get through one step of the repair. It's so frustrating because I'm sitting there thinking, "Come on, just pay attention to this one simple thing," but it's like trying to hold water in your hands. It just slips right through. 

So yeah, I might not remember doing it with a biology textbook in tenth grade because of the whole soccer blur, but I definitely do exactly what you're talking about with repair videos and manuals today. It just doesn't stay in my head.

I just draw a complete blank on that specific stuff. Like, I genuinely don't remember getting in trouble for talking out of turn or anything like that once I got older. I mean, maybe I did? In third grade, you've got one teacher who is basically hovering over you all day, watching every single thing you do. But in high school? You're just one of, like, thirty kids in a room, and honestly, half the teachers looked like they were just trying to survive until the bell rang, too. I would usually just find a desk way in the back row—ideally near the window or the radiator, because I was always freezing after our morning conditioning sessions—and I would just try to be invisible. I wasn't really the loud, disruptive kid anymore because I was just so physically exhausted all the time. I wasn't getting called out, I wasn't getting in trouble... or at least, if I was, I have absolutely zero memory of it. It was all just about doing the bare minimum to get through the hallway and get back out on the field.\n\nDoctor: I want to go back to high school for a second. I mean, don't get me wrong, being unemployed is stressful, obviously, and I do stress about that in general, but in that exact moment when the car drives by? I'm not sitting there panicking about my life or feeling anxious or anything like that. It's literally just... zip, my brain is over there now. \n\nLike, there's zero anxiety attached to the actual distraction. Take the paper towel thing I just mentioned. When I think about the paper towels, I'm not sitting there like, \"Oh my god, what if I spill something and I don't have anything to wipe it up with, what a complete disaster!\" It's nothing like that. It's more just a completely neutral, random observation that suddenly feels like the absolute most important thing in the world to figure out right that second. Like my brain just changes the channel without asking me and suddenly I'm watching a completely different show. \n\nI could be totally relaxed, you know, maybe drinking a cup of coffee, feeling pretty good about the morning, not a worry in the world, and then I'll see, I don't know, a weird bug on the windowsill. And suddenly I'm just looking at the bug. I'm not worried about the bug biting me, I'm not scared of it, I'm just looking at its legs, wondering how it climbs the glass, trying to remember what kind of bugs live in this area. So yeah, to answer your question, it's not anxiety. It's just... my attention gets completely hijacked by whatever shiny new thing enters my orbit, whether it's a sound or a thought or whatever, completely out of nowhere, and worry has nothing to do with it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with concentration and distractibility? Did this start when you were younger, or is this something that's more recent?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing. I mean, I guess I've always been a little scatterbrained, you know? Like in high school, I wasn't exactly the star pupil sitting in the front row taking perfect notes. But it was never... it was never *this* bad. This whole level of just complete inability to stay on track, where it's actually ruining my day and keeping me from getting a job? That really kicked into high gear about, I'd say, a year and ten months ago. \n\nYeah, almost exactly a year and ten months, because that's when I had this really bad soccer injury. I used to play in this adult rec league, right? Just a casual thing, but we played hard, and I ended up completely tearing my ACL. It was awful. I had to have surgery, and the recovery was just brutal. I was basically stuck on the couch for weeks, and then I had months of physical therapy. \n\nAnd before that, I had a really solid routine. I was working, I was playing soccer three nights a week, I was going to the gym on the off days... I was always moving. Always burning off energy, always having somewhere I needed to be at a specific time. But when the injury happened, all of that just stopped. Completely stopped. Suddenly I had zero structure, zero physical outlet, and it was like my brain just didn't know what to do with itself. \n\nI thought once I healed up, things would go back to normal, but they just... didn't. Even after I could walk fine again, the routine was gone, the job was gone, and this crazy distractibility just stayed. It's like the injury knocked me out of my orbit, and I've just been floating around in space ever since, unable to latch onto anything for more than five minutes. So yeah, I'd say it's been about a year and ten months of it being this disruptive.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you used to be very active—playing soccer, going to the gym, always moving around. Since your injury and losing that routine, have you noticed that you feel restless or fidgety a lot? Like, do you find yourself tapping your feet, bouncing your leg, or feeling like you need to move around, even when you're trying to sit still and focus?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Yeah, 100%. It's actually kind of funny you mention that because my buddy was just getting onto me about it the other day. Like, whenever I'm sitting down, especially if I'm sitting with someone else, my leg is just going a mile a minute. Just constantly bouncing up and down. I don't even know I'm doing it until the whole table starts shaking and whoever I'm with is like, \"Dude, chill out,\" and they have to literally put their hand on my knee to get me to stop. \n\nAnd if it's not my leg doing the bouncing, it's my hands. I literally cannot keep my hands empty. If I'm trying to sit still, I'll be clicking a pen—just click, click, click, click—driving everyone in the room crazy. Or I'll have my hand in my pocket just messing with my keys, jingling them around. I have this one keychain, it's like this metal bottle opener thing from my old soccer team, and I'll just sit there flipping it over and over in my fingers without even realizing I'm doing it. \n\nIt's exactly like you said, it's like because I'm not running up and down a field anymore, all that physical energy just got trapped inside my body and it has to leak out somehow, you know? Like a pressure valve or something. So I'm just constantly fidgeting. Even when I'm trying to do those job applications I was telling you about earlier, I'll have a pen in one hand just spinning it or clicking it while I'm trying to read the screen, which obviously doesn't help me focus at all. I just... I don't even realize it's happening. It's completely unconscious. I'll be sitting there, thinking I'm being perfectly relaxed and still, and then I look down and my leg is practically vibrating off the floor. \n\nIt's just so weird how your body does things without your brain giving the okay, right? Like, I'll be at a coffee shop—well, not so much lately since I'm trying to save money since I'm not working, but you know what I mean. If I'm anywhere where I have to just sit and be normal, the bouncing and the clicking and the key jingling just starts up completely on its own.\n\nDoctor: Going back to elementary school and middle school, before high school even—do you remember having trouble paying attention in class, or were teachers ever concerned about you being fidgety or restless back then?\n\nPatient: Oh man, elementary school. Yeah. I mean, I know I said earlier that I was scatterbrained in high school, but honestly, if I really think about it, it goes way, way back before that. \n\nLike, even back in second or third grade, I remember my teachers were just constantly—and I mean *constantly*—moving my seat around the classroom. It was almost like a running joke, except the teachers definitely weren't laughing. They'd start me out in the middle of the room, right? Sitting with a group of desks. But I just could not keep my mouth shut. If a thought popped into my head, I'd just say it out loud, right in the middle of the lesson. I'd just blurt things out, completely out of turn, or I'd be whispering to whoever was sitting next to me about some cartoon I saw or whatever. So then the teacher would sigh and move my desk to the front row. But then I'd just talk to the teacher! So then they'd move me to the very back corner, away from everyone else. \n\nAnd it wasn't just the talking, either. It was the homework. Oh man, my backpack was just this absolute black hole of crumpled up papers. I would just completely forget I even had homework. I'd get to school and everyone's turning in their folders and I'm just sitting there like, \"Wait, we had something to do?\" My mom used to get so frustrated because the teachers were always sending home these little notes about missing assignments. \n\nAnd then when we actually had to do work in class—like, you know those worksheets they'd give you? The ones with like fifty little math problems on them, or the spelling words? I would just blast through them. I just wanted to be done. I couldn't stand just sitting there working on it slowly, so I'd rush through the whole page in like two minutes flat. And then the teacher would hand it back the next day and it would just be covered in red ink. And the frustrating part was, it wasn't because I didn't know the material! It was just completely careless mistakes. Like, I'd write a plus sign instead of a minus, or I'd just completely skip an entire row of questions because my eyes jumped down the page too fast and I didn't even notice. \n\nSo yeah, they were always trying to find the \"perfect\" spot in the room to put my desk where I'd magically stop talking and actually remember my work and slow down, but it never really mattered where they put me. I was always just rushing through those worksheets and making a mess of things. I had this one teacher, Mrs. Gable in third grade, she actually taped a little square on the floor right next to her own desk and that was my spot for like half the year because I was just so all over the place... so yeah, it definitely started way back then, for sure.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to those early years—second, third grade—do you remember if you were bouncing your leg, fidgeting with things in your hands, or having that restless energy even back then, or did that physical restlessness develop later on?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because I’ve been thinking about it... back in second or third grade, I don't actually remember doing the whole leg-bouncing or the pen-clicking thing. But I think... well, I think that’s just because back then, I was always allowed to just run around, you know? You had recess, you had gym class, you rode your bike around the neighborhood until the streetlights came on. I was always just moving my whole body, so the energy never really got backed up, if that makes sense? \n\nBut this current thing, this intense physical restlessness I have right now? That definitely feels like it developed later. Or, well, specifically after the injury. Because, like I was saying, since I can't play soccer like before, that energy is just completely trapped. Sitting around the house makes me feel like I’m literally crawling out of my skin. It’s this horrible, buzzing feeling right under the surface, and I just can't take it, so I end up pacing from room to room. \n\nI mean, I'll just walk from the living room to the kitchen, open the fridge, stare at the ketchup bottle for ten seconds, close it, walk down the hall, look out the bedroom window, and then walk right back to the kitchen. Just doing laps in my own house. My buddy came over the other day to hang out and he actually asked if I was waiting for a package or something because I just could not sit down on the couch with him to watch TV. I just kept pacing back and forth behind the sofa. It's like I'm a caged animal at the zoo or something. \n\nSo yeah, the childhood stuff was more just me being a loud kid who couldn't shut up or stay in his seat, but this crawling-out-of-my-skin, pacing-the-floors kind of restlessness? That's all from losing soccer, 100 percent. I just don't know what to do with my own body anymore when I'm just supposed to be relaxing at home.\n\nDoctor: When you were in high school—before the soccer injury—did you still have that same trouble focusing on homework and tests, or had things improved by that point?\n\nPatient: High school... man, high school is honestly kind of a blur when I try to think about the actual school part of it. Like I said before, I was definitely scatterbrained, but if you're asking if the homework and the tests specifically got better or worse... I really don't know. I can't even picture myself sitting at a desk doing homework back then. \n\nBecause, see, by the time I hit high school, my entire existence—and I mean literally everything I did from the second I woke up to the second I went to sleep—was just completely wrapped up in soccer. We had morning conditioning, then regular classes, then afternoon practice, and then usually film review or something after that. So my brain was just... it was 100 percent on the sport. \n\nI honestly couldn't tell you if my focus in class improved, or if I just sort of figured out how to fly under the radar, you know? Like, the teachers all knew who I was because I was on varsity, and the coaches were always checking our grades to make sure we were eligible to play. So I just did whatever the absolute bare minimum was to keep my GPA exactly where it needed to be. I'd get home at like seven or eight at night, completely dead on my feet, my mom would have this massive plate of food waiting for me—usually pasta or something heavy because of all the calories I was burning—and I'd just eat and pass out. \n\nI don't remember having those same moments of sitting there rushing through fifty math problems like I did in third grade, but I also don't remember ever actually sitting down and studying for a history test, either. I would just kind of show up, stare at the clock, and wait for the bell to ring so I could get back out to the locker room. \n\nSo... yeah, I don't really have a good answer for you on that one. I'm not sure if the trouble focusing went away, or if I just didn't care enough about the classes to even notice I wasn't focusing, if that makes sense? I was just surviving the school day until practice started.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask about middle school specifically—do you remember having trouble keeping up with assignments, or did your teachers ever send notes home about incomplete work or you not paying attention in class back then?\n\nPatient: Middle school... man, that's kind of a weird time to try and remember. Sixth, seventh, eighth grade... you know, it's funny, because elementary school is just so vivid in my mind with the whole desk-moving thing, and then high school, like I was just saying, was just pure, one-hundred-percent soccer. But middle school is kind of this weird, blurry gray area in the middle. \n\nI mean, that's the age when everything changes, right? You're not just in one room all day with one teacher who watches your every move. Suddenly you have, like, six different teachers for different subjects, and you have to navigate the crowded hallways, and you have to remember your locker combination—which, by the way, I was absolutely terrible at. I was always that kid yanking on the metal handle and having to go find the janitor to cut the lock or look up my combination because I just couldn't keep the numbers in my head. \n\nBut as for the actual assignments, or teachers sending notes home about me not paying attention... I honestly couldn't tell you. I just don't have a clear memory of it one way or the other. I mean, I probably was missing things? But I don't remember my mom getting those little physical notes like she did back in third grade. I think maybe by middle school, teachers just don't really send notes home the same way, you know? They expect you to be more independent. \n\nOr maybe... well, I remember they made us buy those planner things. Do you remember those? The school would hand out these little spiral-bound agendas with the school mascot on the front, and they'd tell you to write all your homework down every single day. I would always start off the year with the absolute best intentions. Like, the first week of sixth grade, I'd be writing everything down, maybe even using different colored pens for math and science, really trying to stay on top of it. But then by, like, the second week of October, that planner is just completely buried at the bottom of my locker underneath a bunch of gym shirts and crushed up Doritos bags. \n\nBut yeah, specifically remembering if I was falling behind on assignments or getting called out in class back then... I really just draw a blank. It all just blends together into this awkward phase where I was just trying to figure out how the bells worked and getting really involved in club soccer outside of school. I just draw a complete blank on that specific stuff. Like, I genuinely don't remember getting in trouble for talking out of turn or anything like that once I got older. I mean, maybe I did? But if it happened, it definitely didn't stick in my brain the way the elementary school stuff did. \n\nYou have to remember, by the time you're in high school, the whole dynamic is just completely different. In third grade, you've got one teacher who is basically hovering over you all day, watching every single thing you do. But in high school? You're just one of, like, thirty kids in a room, and honestly, half the teachers looked like they were just trying to survive until the bell rang, too. I would usually just find a desk way in the back row—ideally near the window or the radiator, because I was always freezing after our morning conditioning sessions—and I would just try to be invisible. I wasn't really the loud, disruptive kid anymore because I was just so physically exhausted all the time. When you're running sprints at six in the morning, you don't really have the energy to be talking out of turn in second-period history, you know? I was practically sleeping with my eyes open.\n\nAnd as for the careless mistakes on tests or forgetting assignments... I mean, I don't have any specific memories of a teacher pulling me aside to lecture me about it. They didn't really do that anymore. If I messed up on a test, they'd just hand it back with a red C-minus scribbled on the top and move on to the next kid. And with the missing assignments, it was all just a hustle to stay eligible for the team. If a teacher warned me I was missing something, I'd just scramble and copy a worksheet from one of my teammates in the cafeteria right before class. It was never this big sit-down intervention. \n\nSo, yeah, I just... I don't remember any of that happening in middle or high school. I wasn't getting called out, I wasn't getting in trouble... or at least, if I was, I have absolutely zero memory of it. It was all just about doing the bare minimum to get through the hallway and get back out on the field.\n\nDoctor: I want to go back to high school for a second. Even though you were exhausted from soccer, do you remember struggling to focus during class or on tests back then—like, did you find your mind wandering, or did you have to re-read things multiple times because you weren't paying attention, even when you weren't tired?\n\nPatient: Man, high school... like I said, it's just really hard to separate what was me being physically exhausted from what was just my brain not working right. I'm sure I probably did have to re-read stuff in class, but I just don't have a specific memory of sitting at a desk and doing it back then. \n\nBut, I mean, if you're talking about that specific feeling—where your mind just wanders off and you have to go back over things—I do that constantly right now. Like, all the time. And it's not even with boring school stuff or when I'm tired, it's with things I actually *want* to be doing, when I'm wide awake. \n\nFor example, I like to try and fix things, right? So I can start watching a repair video on YouTube, one of those step-by-step tutorials, or I'll sit down to read the manual for a new tool I just got. I'm genuinely trying to figure out how to use the thing so I don't break it. But after a few minutes, I'll suddenly sort of... snap back to reality, and I realize I've just been staring blankly at it. Like, my eyes are on the screen, the guy in the video is talking, but my brain is completely somewhere else. I haven't processed a single word for the last five minutes. My mind just completely drifted off to something else entirely—like what I'm going to eat later, or some random thought about a car I saw on the street. \n\nSo then I have to stop, grab my phone or the mouse, and back the video up because absolutely none of it stuck. And the crazy part is, sometimes I'll rewind it, start playing it again, and then I'll zone out at the exact same spot! I'll have to rewind the same two-minute clip like three or four times just to get through one step of the repair. It's so frustrating because I'm sitting there thinking, \"Come on, just pay attention to this one simple thing,\" but it's like trying to hold water in your hands. It just slips right through. \n\nSo yeah, I might not remember doing it with a biology textbook in tenth grade because of the whole soccer blur, but I definitely do exactly what you're talking about with repair videos and manuals today. It just doesn't stay in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Sixth, seventh, eighth grade... you know, it's funny, because elementary school is just so vivid in my mind with the whole desk-moving thing, and then high school, like I was just saying, was just pure, one-hundred-percent soccer. So I can start watching a repair video on YouTube, one of those step-by-step tutorials, or I'll sit down to read the manual for a new tool I just got. I'm genuinely trying to figure out how to use the thing so I don't break it. But after a few minutes, I'll suddenly sort of... snap back to reality, and I realize I've just been staring blankly at it. Like, my eyes are on the screen, the guy in the video is talking, but my brain is completely somewhere else. I haven't processed a single word for the last five minutes. My mind just completely drifted off to something else entirely—like what I'm going to eat later, or some random thought about a car I saw on the street. \n\nSo then I have to stop, grab my phone or the mouse, and back the video up because absolutely none of it stuck. And the crazy part is, sometimes I'll rewind it, start playing it again, and then I'll zone out at the exact same spot! I'll have to rewind the same two-minute clip like three or four times just to get through one step of the repair. It's so frustrating because I'm sitting there thinking, \"Come on, just pay attention to this one simple thing,\" but it's like trying to hold water in your hands. It just slips right through. \n\nSo yeah, I might not remember doing it with a biology textbook in tenth grade because of the whole soccer blur, but I definitely do exactly what you're talking about with repair videos and manuals today. It just doesn't stay in my head.\n\nDoctor: I know high school is a blur with all the soccer, but let me ask it differently: In middle school, before you got really deep into competitive soccer—say, sixth or seventh grade—do you remember your parents or teachers ever having to remind you to do your homework, or do you remember losing or forgetting assignments the way you did in elementary school?\n\nPatient: I know you're really trying to zero in on that specific middle school window, like right before the soccer really took over my entire life, but honestly... I just keep coming up completely empty when I try to picture it. Sixth and seventh grade, it’s just... I mean, maybe she did? She was always pretty involved when I was younger, like I told you with those physical notes in elementary school, but by middle school, I think my parents were kind of just assuming I had it figured out, or maybe they were just distracted. My dad was working crazy hours back then, and my mom was always running around doing errands and just trying to keep the house together, so I don't really remember them hovering over me or constantly nagging me to get my homework done. \n\nAnd as for losing assignments... well, I mean, I told you earlier about my locker being an absolute disaster zone with the gym clothes and the crushed-up Doritos bags, so logically, I'm sure things got lost. But do I actually have a specific memory of a teacher standing at the front of the room, calling my name, and me panicking because I realized I lost the homework? I just don't. \n\nIt’s actually kind of frustrating because I really want to give you a solid \"yes\" or \"no\" to help you piece this all together, but that whole era is just a giant gap in my memory. It’s like... elementary school, I clearly remember the desk moving. High school, I remember the physical exhaustion and the soccer. But those middle years? It's just a complete wash. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
You know, it’s funny you ask about sitting down for thirty minutes, because I’m sitting here really trying to picture my actual bedroom from back then to see if it triggers anything. We lived in this older split-level house at the time, and my room was right down the short hallway from the living room. I had this little wooden desk—it was actually a hand-me-down from my older cousin, and it had these weird, clunky carved handles that would always get stuck when you tried to open the drawers—and I remember sitting there. I can picture the desk perfectly. I even remember this little metal desk lamp I had that would get ridiculously hot if you left it on for more than twenty minutes. You'd literally burn your hand if you bumped into it. 

But if you’re asking me if I actually sat there and focused on a math worksheet for a solid half hour? Or if my mind was wandering and I kept having to start over? I... I honestly just couldn't tell you. It’s like I can picture the exact setting, the room, the desk, the hot lamp, but the actual act of doing the homework is just... it's just not there. I don't know if I stared at the wall for an hour, or if I somehow knocked it out in ten minutes, or if I was constantly getting up to go to the kitchen to get a snack. 

I feel like maybe I used to do it on the floor sometimes, too, instead of the desk? Like, maybe while the TV was on in the other room? I can sort of imagine hearing the local news or whatever my parents were watching echoing down the hall while I had my books spread out on the carpet. But trying to remember the specific feeling of being distracted or having to restart my work back in sixth or seventh grade... I just don't have it. I know exactly how my brain works right now, with the YouTube videos and all that, but back then? It’s just a complete blank. I really wish I could give you a clear yes or no on the thirty minutes thing, but I just can't pull up a single memory of how I actually functioned when I sat down to do it.

But I just could not keep my mouth shut. If a thought popped into my head, I'd just say it out loud, right in the middle of the lesson. And with the missing assignments, it was all just a hustle to stay eligible for the team. If a teacher warned me I was missing something, I'd just scramble and copy a worksheet from one of my teammates in the cafeteria right before class. I wasn't getting called out, I wasn't getting in trouble... or at least, if I was, I have absolutely zero memory of it. It was all just about doing the bare minimum to get through the hallway and get back out on the field.\n\nDoctor: I want to go back to high school for a second. Even though you were exhausted from soccer, do you remember struggling to focus during class or on tests back then—like, did you find your mind wandering, or did you have to re-read things multiple times because you weren't paying attention, even when you weren't tired?\n\nPatient: Man, high school... like I said, it's just really hard to separate what was me being physically exhausted from what was just my brain not working right. Like, all the time.
