So I can start watching a repair video on YouTube, one of those step-by-step tutorials, or I'll sit down to read the manual for a new tool I just got. I'm genuinely trying to figure out how to use the thing so I don't break it. But after a few minutes, I'll suddenly sort of... snap back to reality, and I realize I've just been staring blankly at it. Like, my eyes are on the screen, the guy in the video is talking, but my brain is completely somewhere else. I haven't processed a single word for the last five minutes. My mind just completely drifted off to something else entirely—like what I'm going to eat later, or some random thought about a car I saw on the street. \n\nSo then I have to stop, grab my phone or the mouse, and back the video up because absolutely none of it stuck. And the crazy part is, sometimes I'll rewind it, start playing it again, and then I'll zone out at the exact same spot! I'll have to rewind the same two-minute clip like three or four times just to get through one step of the repair. It's so frustrating because I'm sitting there thinking, \"Come on, just pay attention to this one simple thing,\" but it's like trying to hold water in your hands. It just slips right through. \n\nSo yeah, I might not remember doing it with a biology textbook in tenth grade because of the whole soccer blur, but I definitely do exactly what you're talking about with repair videos and manuals today. It just doesn't stay in my head.\n\nDoctor: I know high school is a blur with all the soccer, but let me ask it differently: In middle school, before you got really deep into competitive soccer—say, sixth or seventh grade—do you remember your parents or teachers ever having to remind you to do your homework, or do you remember losing or forgetting assignments the way you did in elementary school?\n\nPatient: I know you're really trying to zero in on that specific middle school window, like right before the soccer really took over my entire life, but honestly... I just keep coming up completely empty when I try to picture it. Sixth and seventh grade, it’s just... I don't know. \n\nLike, I try to picture my mom sitting me down at the kitchen table—we had this big, heavy oak table back then where we ate dinner every night—and I try to picture her standing over me saying, \"Did you do your math?\" or \"Where is your science worksheet?\" but the image just isn't there. I mean, maybe she did? She was always pretty involved when I was younger, like I told you with those physical notes in elementary school, but by middle school, I think my parents were kind of just assuming I had it figured out, or maybe they were just distracted. My dad was working crazy hours back then, and my mom was always running around doing errands and just trying to keep the house together, so I don't really remember them hovering over me or constantly nagging me to get my homework done. \n\nAnd as for losing assignments... well, I mean, I told you earlier about my locker being an absolute disaster zone with the gym clothes and the crushed-up Doritos bags, so logically, I'm sure things got lost. It was basically a black hole in there. You'd put a permission slip or a folder in there and it would just vanish into another dimension. But do I actually have a specific memory of a teacher standing at the front of the room, calling my name, and me panicking because I realized I lost the homework? I just don't. \n\nIt’s actually kind of frustrating because I really want to give you a solid \"yes\" or \"no\" to help you piece this all together, but that whole era is just a giant gap in my memory. It’s like... elementary school, I clearly remember the desk moving. High school, I remember the physical exhaustion and the soccer. But those middle years? It's just a complete wash. I really wish I could give you a better answer, but I just don't remember them having to remind me, or me losing things back then. If it happened, it just completely faded out of my brain.\n\nDoctor: I understand middle school is fuzzy, but let me ask it this way: When you were in sixth or seventh grade, if you sat down to do homework, could you actually sit there and focus on it for, say, thirty minutes, or did you find yourself getting distracted and having to start over multiple times—even back then, before soccer became everything?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask about sitting down for thirty minutes, because I’m sitting here really trying to picture my actual bedroom from back then to see if it triggers anything. We lived in this older split-level house at the time, and my room was right down the short hallway from the living room. I had this little wooden desk—it was actually a hand-me-down from my older cousin, and it had these weird, clunky carved handles that would always get stuck when you tried to open the drawers—and I remember sitting there. I can picture the desk perfectly. I even remember this little metal desk lamp I had that would get ridiculously hot if you left it on for more than twenty minutes. You'd literally burn your hand if you bumped into it. \n\nBut if you’re asking me if I actually sat there and focused on a math worksheet for a solid half hour? Or if my mind was wandering and I kept having to start over? I... I honestly just couldn't tell you. It’s like I can picture the exact setting, the room, the desk, the hot lamp, but the actual act of doing the homework is just... it's just not there. I don't know if I stared at the wall for an hour, or if I somehow knocked it out in ten minutes, or if I was constantly getting up to go to the kitchen to get a snack. \n\nI feel like maybe I used to do it on the floor sometimes, too, instead of the desk? Like, maybe while the TV was on in the other room? I can sort of imagine hearing the local news or whatever my parents were watching echoing down the hall while I had my books spread out on the carpet. But trying to remember the specific feeling of being distracted or having to restart my work back in sixth or seventh grade... I just don't have it. I know exactly how my brain works right now, with the YouTube videos and all that, but back then? It’s just a complete blank. I really wish I could give you a clear yes or no on the thirty minutes thing, but I just can't pull up a single memory of how I actually functioned when I sat down to do it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I'm not worried about the bug biting me, I'm not scared of it, I'm just looking at its legs, wondering how it climbs the glass, trying to remember what kind of bugs live in this area. So yeah, to answer your question, it's not anxiety. It's just... my attention gets completely hijacked by whatever shiny new thing enters my orbit, whether it's a sound or a thought or whatever, completely out of nowhere, and worry has nothing to do with it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with concentration and distractibility? Did this start when you were younger, or is this something that's more recent?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing. I mean, I guess I've always been a little scatterbrained, you know? Like in high school, I wasn't exactly the star pupil sitting in the front row taking perfect notes. But it was never... it was never *this* bad. This whole level of just complete inability to stay on track, where it's actually ruining my day and keeping me from getting a job? That really kicked into high gear about, I'd say, a year and ten months ago. \n\nYeah, almost exactly a year and ten months, because that's when I had this really bad soccer injury. I used to play in this adult rec league, right? Just a casual thing, but we played hard, and I ended up completely tearing my ACL. It was awful. I had to have surgery, and the recovery was just brutal. I was basically stuck on the couch for weeks, and then I had months of physical therapy. \n\nAnd before that, I had a really solid routine. I was working, I was playing soccer three nights a week, I was going to the gym on the off days... I was always moving. Always burning off energy, always having somewhere I needed to be at a specific time. But when the injury happened, all of that just stopped. Completely stopped. Suddenly I had zero structure, zero physical outlet, and it was like my brain just didn't know what to do with itself. \n\nI thought once I healed up, things would go back to normal, but they just... didn't. Even after I could walk fine again, the routine was gone, the job was gone, and this crazy distractibility just stayed. It's like the injury knocked me out of my orbit, and I've just been floating around in space ever since, unable to latch onto anything for more than five minutes. So yeah, I'd say it's been about a year and ten months of it being this disruptive.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you used to be very active—playing soccer, going to the gym, always moving around. Since your injury and losing that routine, have you noticed that you feel restless or fidgety a lot? Like, do you find yourself tapping your feet, bouncing your leg, or feeling like you need to move around, even when you're trying to sit still and focus?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Yeah, 100%. It's actually kind of funny you mention that because my buddy was just getting onto me about it the other day. Like, whenever I'm sitting down, especially if I'm sitting with someone else, my leg is just going a mile a minute. Just constantly bouncing up and down. I don't even know I'm doing it until the whole table starts shaking and whoever I'm with is like, \"Dude, chill out,\" and they have to literally put their hand on my knee to get me to stop. \n\nAnd if it's not my leg doing the bouncing, it's my hands. I literally cannot keep my hands empty. If I'm trying to sit still, I'll be clicking a pen—just click, click, click, click—driving everyone in the room crazy. Or I'll have my hand in my pocket just messing with my keys, jingling them around. I have this one keychain, it's like this metal bottle opener thing from my old soccer team, and I'll just sit there flipping it over and over in my fingers without even realizing I'm doing it. \n\nIt's exactly like you said, it's like because I'm not running up and down a field anymore, all that physical energy just got trapped inside my body and it has to leak out somehow, you know? Like a pressure valve or something. So I'm just constantly fidgeting. Even when I'm trying to do those job applications I was telling you about earlier, I'll have a pen in one hand just spinning it or clicking it while I'm trying to read the screen, which obviously doesn't help me focus at all. I just... I don't even realize it's happening. It's completely unconscious. I'll be sitting there, thinking I'm being perfectly relaxed and still, and then I look down and my leg is practically vibrating off the floor. \n\nIt's just so weird how your body does things without your brain giving the okay, right? Like, I'll be at a coffee shop—well, not so much lately since I'm trying to save money since I'm not working, but you know what I mean. If I'm anywhere where I have to just sit and be normal, the bouncing and the clicking and the key jingling just starts up completely on its own.\n\nDoctor: Going back to elementary school and middle school, before high school even—do you remember having trouble paying attention in class, or were teachers ever concerned about you being fidgety or restless back then?\n\nPatient: Oh man, elementary school. Yeah. I mean, I know I said earlier that I was scatterbrained in high school, but honestly, if I really think about it, it goes way, way back before that. \n\nLike, even back in second or third grade, I remember my teachers were just constantly—and I mean *constantly*—moving my seat around the classroom. It was almost like a running joke, except the teachers definitely weren't laughing. They'd start me out in the middle of the room, right? Sitting with a group of desks. But I just could not keep my mouth shut. If a thought popped into my head, I'd just say it out loud, right in the middle of the lesson. I'd just blurt things out, completely out of turn, or I'd be whispering to whoever was sitting next to me about some cartoon I saw or whatever. So then the teacher would sigh and move my desk to the front row. But then I'd just talk to the teacher! So then they'd move me to the very back corner, away from everyone else. \n\nAnd it wasn't just the talking, either. It was the homework. Oh man, my backpack was just this absolute black hole of crumpled up papers. I would just completely forget I even had homework. I'd get to school and everyone's turning in their folders and I'm just sitting there like, \"Wait, we had something to do?\" My mom used to get so frustrated because the teachers were always sending home these little notes about missing assignments. \n\nAnd then when we actually had to do work in class—like, you know those worksheets they'd give you? The ones with like fifty little math problems on them, or the spelling words? I would just blast through them. I just wanted to be done. I couldn't stand just sitting there working on it slowly, so I'd rush through the whole page in like two minutes flat. And then the teacher would hand it back the next day and it would just be covered in red ink. And the frustrating part was, it wasn't because I didn't know the material! It was just completely careless mistakes. Like, I'd write a plus sign instead of a minus, or I'd just completely skip an entire row of questions because my eyes jumped down the page too fast and I didn't even notice. \n\nSo yeah, they were always trying to find the \"perfect\" spot in the room to put my desk where I'd magically stop talking and actually remember my work and slow down, but it never really mattered where they put me. I was always just rushing through those worksheets and making a mess of things. I had this one teacher, Mrs. Gable in third grade, she actually taped a little square on the floor right next to her own desk and that was my spot for like half the year because I was just so all over the place... so yeah, it definitely started way back then, for sure.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to those early years—second, third grade—do you remember if you were bouncing your leg, fidgeting with things in your hands, or having that restless energy even back then, or did that physical restlessness develop later on?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because I’ve been thinking about it... back in second or third grade, I don't actually remember doing the whole leg-bouncing or the pen-clicking thing. You had recess, you had gym class, you rode your bike around the neighborhood until the streetlights came on. My buddy came over the other day to hang out and he actually asked if I was waiting for a package or something because I just could not sit down on the couch with him to watch TV. We had morning conditioning, then regular classes, then afternoon practice, and then usually film review or something after that. So my brain was just... it was 100 percent on the sport. \n\nI honestly couldn't tell you if my focus in class improved, or if I just sort of figured out how to fly under the radar, you know? Like, the teachers all knew who I was because I was on varsity, and the coaches were always checking our grades to make sure we were eligible to play. I'd get home at like seven or eight at night, completely dead on my feet, my mom would have this massive plate of food waiting for me—usually pasta or something heavy because of all the calories I was burning—and I'd just eat and pass out. \n\nI don't remember having those same moments of sitting there rushing through fifty math problems like I did in third grade, but I also don't remember ever actually sitting down and studying for a history test, either. You're not just in one room all day with one teacher who watches your every move. Man, you are really digging into the middle school vault today. Grades... okay, let me try to think about report cards back then. We used to get them mailed directly to the house at the end of every quarter, and they always came in those little white window envelopes. I remember my mom would usually be the one to open them because she always grabbed the mail from the box at the end of the driveway first thing. She would stand in the kitchen, usually right by the landline phone—we had this massive, ridiculously long curly cord on it that could stretch all the way across the room to the fridge—and she would open the envelope right there. 

But as for her actually expressing concern, or my dad sitting me down because I wasn't keeping up? I just don't think so. Or at least, I have zero memory of it. I feel like if I was totally bombing out and failing my classes, I would definitely remember the fallout from that, right? Like, you remember getting grounded or losing your privileges. I had a PlayStation 2 back then, and I played it constantly, so if they had taken that away because of my grades, that would be burned into my brain. But I don't remember any big, dramatic interventions or them telling me I needed a tutor or anything like that. 

And with the teachers, it was kind of a different vibe than elementary school. That was the first time we had to switch rooms for every subject, so instead of one teacher who knew everything about you, you were just one of like thirty kids cycling through their room for forty-five minutes. So maybe I was just flying under the radar. Maybe I was just pulling solid C-minuses and blending in with the background, so nobody felt the need to sound the alarm. I really don't remember any teacher ever holding me after the bell rang to tell me they were worried about my academic progress. If they did, or if my parents were secretly stressing out about it behind closed doors, they kept it from me, because I just don't have any memory of anyone being concerned about it back then.

I'd get home at like seven or eight at night, completely dead on my feet, my mom would have this massive plate of food waiting for me—usually pasta or something heavy because of all the calories I was burning—and I'd just eat and pass out. \n\nI don't remember having those same moments of sitting there rushing through fifty math problems like I did in third grade, but I also don't remember ever actually sitting down and studying for a history test, either. I would just kind of show up, stare at the clock, and wait for the bell to ring so I could get back out to the locker room. \n\nSo... yeah, I don't really have a good answer for you on that one. I'm not sure if the trouble focusing went away, or if I just didn't care enough about the classes to even notice I wasn't focusing, if that makes sense? I was just surviving the school day until practice started.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask about middle school specifically—do you remember having trouble keeping up with assignments, or did your teachers ever send notes home about incomplete work or you not paying attention in class back then?\n\nPatient: Middle school... man, that's kind of a weird time to try and remember. Sixth, seventh, eighth grade... you know, it's funny, because elementary school is just so vivid in my mind with the whole desk-moving thing, and then high school, like I was just saying, was just pure, one-hundred-percent soccer. But middle school is kind of this weird, blurry gray area in the middle. \n\nI mean, that's the age when everything changes, right? You're not just in one room all day with one teacher who watches your every move. Suddenly you have, like, six different teachers for different subjects, and you have to navigate the crowded hallways, and you have to remember your locker combination—which, by the way, I was absolutely terrible at. I was always that kid yanking on the metal handle and having to go find the janitor to cut the lock or look up my combination because I just couldn't keep the numbers in my head. \n\nBut as for the actual assignments, or teachers sending notes home about me not paying attention... I honestly couldn't tell you. I just don't have a clear memory of it one way or the other. I mean, I probably was missing things? But I don't remember my mom getting those little physical notes like she did back in third grade. I think maybe by middle school, teachers just don't really send notes home the same way, you know? They expect you to be more independent. \n\nOr maybe... well, I remember they made us buy those planner things. Do you remember those? The school would hand out these little spiral-bound agendas with the school mascot on the front, and they'd tell you to write all your homework down every single day. I would always start off the year with the absolute best intentions. Like, the first week of sixth grade, I'd be writing everything down, maybe even using different colored pens for math and science, really trying to stay on top of it. But then by, like, the second week of October, that planner is just completely buried at the bottom of my locker underneath a bunch of gym shirts and crushed up Doritos bags. \n\nBut yeah, specifically remembering if I was falling behind on assignments or getting called out in class back then... I really just draw a blank. It all just blends together into this awkward phase where I was just trying to figure out how the bells worked and getting really involved in club soccer outside of school. So I don't really have a specific answer for you on the middle school stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to middle school and high school, before the soccer injury—aside from just being busy with soccer—do you remember ever getting in trouble with teachers for talking out of turn, or forgetting to turn in assignments, or making careless mistakes on tests the way you did back in elementary school?\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to piece together that timeline, and I really wish I could give you a better picture, but... I just draw a complete blank on that specific stuff. Like, I genuinely don't remember getting in trouble for talking out of turn or anything like that once I got older. I mean, maybe I did? But if it happened, it definitely didn't stick in my brain the way the elementary school stuff did. \n\nYou have to remember, by the time you're in high school, the whole dynamic is just completely different. In third grade, you've got one teacher who is basically hovering over you all day, watching every single thing you do. But in high school? You're just one of, like, thirty kids in a room, and honestly, half the teachers looked like they were just trying to survive until the bell rang, too. I would usually just find a desk way in the back row—ideally near the window or the radiator, because I was always freezing after our morning conditioning sessions—and I would just try to be invisible. I wasn't really the loud, disruptive kid anymore because I was just so physically exhausted all the time. When you're running sprints at six in the morning, you don't really have the energy to be talking out of turn in second-period history, you know? I was practically sleeping with my eyes open.\n\nAnd as for the careless mistakes on tests or forgetting assignments... I mean, I don't have any specific memories of a teacher pulling me aside to lecture me about it. They didn't really do that anymore. If I messed up on a test, they'd just hand it back with a red C-minus scribbled on the top and move on to the next kid. And with the missing assignments, it was all just a hustle to stay eligible for the team. If a teacher warned me I was missing something, I'd just scramble and copy a worksheet from one of my teammates in the cafeteria right before class. It was never this big sit-down intervention. \n\nSo, yeah, I just... I don't remember any of that happening in middle or high school. I wasn't getting called out, I wasn't getting in trouble... or at least, if I was, I have absolutely zero memory of it. It was all just about doing the bare minimum to get through the hallway and get back out on the field.\n\nDoctor: I want to go back to high school for a second. Even though you were exhausted from soccer, do you remember struggling to focus during class or on tests back then—like, did you find your mind wandering, or did you have to re-read things multiple times because you weren't paying attention, even when you weren't tired?\n\nPatient: Man, high school... like I said, it's just really hard to separate what was me being physically exhausted from what was just my brain not working right. I'm sure I probably did have to re-read stuff in class, but I just don't have a specific memory of sitting at a desk and doing it back then. \n\nBut, I mean, if you're talking about that specific feeling—where your mind just wanders off and you have to go back over things—I do that constantly right now. Like, all the time. And it's not even with boring school stuff or when I'm tired, it's with things I actually *want* to be doing, when I'm wide awake. \n\nFor example, I like to try and fix things, right? So I can start watching a repair video on YouTube, one of those step-by-step tutorials, or I'll sit down to read the manual for a new tool I just got. I'm genuinely trying to figure out how to use the thing so I don't break it. But after a few minutes, I'll suddenly sort of... snap back to reality, and I realize I've just been staring blankly at it. Like, my eyes are on the screen, the guy in the video is talking, but my brain is completely somewhere else. I haven't processed a single word for the last five minutes. My mind just completely drifted off to something else entirely—like what I'm going to eat later, or some random thought about a car I saw on the street. \n\nSo then I have to stop, grab my phone or the mouse, and back the video up because absolutely none of it stuck. And the crazy part is, sometimes I'll rewind it, start playing it again, and then I'll zone out at the exact same spot! I'll have to rewind the same two-minute clip like three or four times just to get through one step of the repair. It's so frustrating because I'm sitting there thinking, \"Come on, just pay attention to this one simple thing,\" but it's like trying to hold water in your hands. It just slips right through. \n\nSo yeah, I might not remember doing it with a biology textbook in tenth grade because of the whole soccer blur, but I definitely do exactly what you're talking about with repair videos and manuals today. It just doesn't stay in my head.\n\nDoctor: I know high school is a blur with all the soccer, but let me ask it differently: In middle school, before you got really deep into competitive soccer—say, sixth or seventh grade—do you remember your parents or teachers ever having to remind you to do your homework, or do you remember losing or forgetting assignments the way you did in elementary school?\n\nPatient: I know you're really trying to zero in on that specific middle school window, like right before the soccer really took over my entire life, but honestly... I just keep coming up completely empty when I try to picture it. Sixth and seventh grade, it’s just... I don't know. \n\nLike, I try to picture my mom sitting me down at the kitchen table—we had this big, heavy oak table back then where we ate dinner every night—and I try to picture her standing over me saying, \"Did you do your math?\" or \"Where is your science worksheet?\" but the image just isn't there. I mean, maybe she did? She was always pretty involved when I was younger, like I told you with those physical notes in elementary school, but by middle school, I think my parents were kind of just assuming I had it figured out, or maybe they were just distracted. My dad was working crazy hours back then, and my mom was always running around doing errands and just trying to keep the house together, so I don't really remember them hovering over me or constantly nagging me to get my homework done. \n\nAnd as for losing assignments... well, I mean, I told you earlier about my locker being an absolute disaster zone with the gym clothes and the crushed-up Doritos bags, so logically, I'm sure things got lost. It was basically a black hole in there. You'd put a permission slip or a folder in there and it would just vanish into another dimension. But do I actually have a specific memory of a teacher standing at the front of the room, calling my name, and me panicking because I realized I lost the homework? I just don't. \n\nIt’s actually kind of frustrating because I really want to give you a solid \"yes\" or \"no\" to help you piece this all together, but that whole era is just a giant gap in my memory. It’s like... elementary school, I clearly remember the desk moving. High school, I remember the physical exhaustion and the soccer. But those middle years? It's just a complete wash. I really wish I could give you a better answer, but I just don't remember them having to remind me, or me losing things back then. If it happened, it just completely faded out of my brain.\n\nDoctor: I understand middle school is fuzzy, but let me ask it this way: When you were in sixth or seventh grade, if you sat down to do homework, could you actually sit there and focus on it for, say, thirty minutes, or did you find yourself getting distracted and having to start over multiple times—even back then, before soccer became everything?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask about sitting down for thirty minutes, because I’m sitting here really trying to picture my actual bedroom from back then to see if it triggers anything. We lived in this older split-level house at the time, and my room was right down the short hallway from the living room. I had this little wooden desk—it was actually a hand-me-down from my older cousin, and it had these weird, clunky carved handles that would always get stuck when you tried to open the drawers—and I remember sitting there. I can picture the desk perfectly. I even remember this little metal desk lamp I had that would get ridiculously hot if you left it on for more than twenty minutes. You'd literally burn your hand if you bumped into it. \n\nBut if you’re asking me if I actually sat there and focused on a math worksheet for a solid half hour? Or if my mind was wandering and I kept having to start over? I... I honestly just couldn't tell you. It’s like I can picture the exact setting, the room, the desk, the hot lamp, but the actual act of doing the homework is just... it's just not there. I don't know if I stared at the wall for an hour, or if I somehow knocked it out in ten minutes, or if I was constantly getting up to go to the kitchen to get a snack. \n\nI feel like maybe I used to do it on the floor sometimes, too, instead of the desk? Like, maybe while the TV was on in the other room? I can sort of imagine hearing the local news or whatever my parents were watching echoing down the hall while I had my books spread out on the carpet. But trying to remember the specific feeling of being distracted or having to restart my work back in sixth or seventh grade... I just don't have it. I know exactly how my brain works right now, with the YouTube videos and all that, but back then? It’s just a complete blank. I really wish I could give you a clear yes or no on the thirty minutes thing, but I just can't pull up a single memory of how I actually functioned when I sat down to do it.\n\nDoctor: I know you can't remember every detail, but let me ask you this: Did your parents or teachers ever express concern about your grades or your ability to keep up academically in middle school, even before soccer became such a big part of your life?\n\nPatient: Man, you are really digging into the middle school vault today. We used to get them mailed directly to the house at the end of every quarter, and they always came in those little white window envelopes. I remember my mom would usually be the one to open them because she always grabbed the mail from the box at the end of the driveway first thing. She would stand in the kitchen, usually right by the landline phone—we had this massive, ridiculously long curly cord on it that could stretch all the way across the room to the fridge—and she would open the envelope right there. \n\nBut as for her actually expressing concern, or my dad sitting me down because I wasn't keeping up? I just don't think so. Or at least, I have zero memory of it. I feel like if I was totally bombing out and failing my classes, I would definitely remember the fallout from that, right? Like, you remember getting grounded or losing your privileges. But I don't remember any big, dramatic interventions or them telling me I needed a tutor or anything like that. \n\nAnd with the teachers, it was kind of a different vibe than elementary school. That was the first time we had to switch rooms for every subject, so instead of one teacher who knew everything about you, you were just one of like thirty kids cycling through their room for forty-five minutes. So maybe I was just flying under the radar. Maybe I was just pulling solid C-minuses and blending in with the background, so nobody felt the need to sound the alarm. I really don't remember any teacher ever holding me after the bell rang to tell me they were worried about my academic progress. If they did, or if my parents were secretly stressing out about it behind closed doors, they kept it from me, because I just don't have any memory of anyone being concerned about it back then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, when you compare it to the job applications... I mean, the way it happens now is just so frustrating, because the main thing is I get pulled off track by every little sound or thought. It’s like I have zero filter for the rest of the world. I’ll be sitting there at my laptop, trying to work on an application—typing in my address, trying to remember the exact months I worked at my last job—and then a car will drive by outside. Not even a loud car, just tires on the wet pavement. Or my phone will vibrate on the desk, or, honestly, it doesn't even have to be a real sound. It could just be some random idea that pops into my head, like wondering if I left my wet towel on the bed or trying to remember the name of an actor from a movie I watched three weeks ago. And that just sends me somewhere else entirely before I even notice it's happening. I don't make a conscious choice to stop working on the application, I just suddenly snap back to reality and realize I've been watching YouTube videos about restoring old cast iron skillets for an hour. 

But back in second or third grade? Did I do that? I mean, obviously I wasn't falling down internet rabbit holes, but... yeah, I think the actual feeling of it was exactly the same. 

I remember my third-grade classroom had those individual desks that the teacher would push together into little groups of four or five, you know, to make little pods? And if we were supposed to be doing a spelling worksheet, I wouldn't be doing it, because I'd hear the radiator clanking by the window. Or someone across the room would get up to use the pencil sharpener—one of those old, heavy metal crank ones bolted to the wall that made that awful grinding noise—and my brain would just instantly lock onto that sound. Then I'd be watching them sharpen the pencil, and then I'd look past them out the window at the playground, and I'd start thinking about recess, and I'd just be completely gone in my own head. 

So I wasn't losing an hour to a phone, because I didn't have one, but I was definitely getting hijacked by every little distraction in that classroom before I even realized I wasn't doing my work. The teacher would say time was up, and I'd look down, and I had maybe written my name at the top of the paper. So yeah, the stuff distracting me has changed, but that immediate pull away from whatever I'm supposed to be focusing on? That was definitely happening back then, too.

I just draw a complete blank on that specific stuff. Like, I genuinely don't remember getting in trouble for talking out of turn or anything like that once I got older. I mean, maybe I did? But if it happened, it definitely didn't stick in my brain the way the elementary school stuff did. \n\nYou have to remember, by the time you're in high school, the whole dynamic is just completely different. In third grade, you've got one teacher who is basically hovering over you all day, watching every single thing you do. But in high school? You're just one of, like, thirty kids in a room, and honestly, half the teachers looked like they were just trying to survive until the bell rang, too. I would usually just find a desk way in the back row—ideally near the window or the radiator, because I was always freezing after our morning conditioning sessions—and I would just try to be invisible. I wasn't really the loud, disruptive kid anymore because I was just so physically exhausted all the time. When you're running sprints at six in the morning, you don't really have the energy to be talking out of turn in second-period history, you know? I was practically sleeping with my eyes open.\n\nAnd as for the careless mistakes on tests or forgetting assignments... I mean, I don't have any specific memories of a teacher pulling me aside to lecture me about it. They didn't really do that anymore. If I messed up on a test, they'd just hand it back with a red C-minus scribbled on the top and move on to the next kid. And with the missing assignments, it was all just a hustle to stay eligible for the team. If a teacher warned me I was missing something, I'd just scramble and copy a worksheet from one of my teammates in the cafeteria right before class. It was never this big sit-down intervention. \n\nSo, yeah, I just... I don't remember any of that happening in middle or high school. I wasn't getting called out, I wasn't getting in trouble... or at least, if I was, I have absolutely zero memory of it. It was all just about doing the bare minimum to get through the hallway and get back out on the field.\n\nDoctor: I want to go back to high school for a second. Even though you were exhausted from soccer, do you remember struggling to focus during class or on tests back then—like, did you find your mind wandering, or did you have to re-read things multiple times because you weren't paying attention, even when you weren't tired?\n\nPatient: Man, high school... like I said, it's just really hard to separate what was me being physically exhausted from what was just my brain not working right. I'm sure I probably did have to re-read stuff in class, but I just don't have a specific memory of sitting at a desk and doing it back then. \n\nBut, I mean, if you're talking about that specific feeling—where your mind just wanders off and you have to go back over things—I do that constantly right now. Like, all the time. And it's not even with boring school stuff or when I'm tired, it's with things I actually *want* to be doing, when I'm wide awake. \n\nFor example, I like to try and fix things, right? So I can start watching a repair video on YouTube, one of those step-by-step tutorials, or I'll sit down to read the manual for a new tool I just got. I'm genuinely trying to figure out how to use the thing so I don't break it. But after a few minutes, I'll suddenly sort of... snap back to reality, and I realize I've just been staring blankly at it. Like, my eyes are on the screen, the guy in the video is talking, but my brain is completely somewhere else. I haven't processed a single word for the last five minutes. My mind just completely drifted off to something else entirely—like what I'm going to eat later, or some random thought about a car I saw on the street. \n\nSo then I have to stop, grab my phone or the mouse, and back the video up because absolutely none of it stuck. And the crazy part is, sometimes I'll rewind it, start playing it again, and then I'll zone out at the exact same spot! I'll have to rewind the same two-minute clip like three or four times just to get through one step of the repair. It's so frustrating because I'm sitting there thinking, \"Come on, just pay attention to this one simple thing,\" but it's like trying to hold water in your hands. It just slips right through. \n\nSo yeah, I might not remember doing it with a biology textbook in tenth grade because of the whole soccer blur, but I definitely do exactly what you're talking about with repair videos and manuals today. It just doesn't stay in my head.\n\nDoctor: I know high school is a blur with all the soccer, but let me ask it differently: In middle school, before you got really deep into competitive soccer—say, sixth or seventh grade—do you remember your parents or teachers ever having to remind you to do your homework, or do you remember losing or forgetting assignments the way you did in elementary school?\n\nPatient: I know you're really trying to zero in on that specific middle school window, like right before the soccer really took over my entire life, but honestly... I just keep coming up completely empty when I try to picture it. Sixth and seventh grade, it’s just... I don't know. \n\nLike, I try to picture my mom sitting me down at the kitchen table—we had this big, heavy oak table back then where we ate dinner every night—and I try to picture her standing over me saying, \"Did you do your math?\" or \"Where is your science worksheet?\" but the image just isn't there. I mean, maybe she did? She was always pretty involved when I was younger, like I told you with those physical notes in elementary school, but by middle school, I think my parents were kind of just assuming I had it figured out, or maybe they were just distracted. My dad was working crazy hours back then, and my mom was always running around doing errands and just trying to keep the house together, so I don't really remember them hovering over me or constantly nagging me to get my homework done. \n\nAnd as for losing assignments... well, I mean, I told you earlier about my locker being an absolute disaster zone with the gym clothes and the crushed-up Doritos bags, so logically, I'm sure things got lost. It was basically a black hole in there. You'd put a permission slip or a folder in there and it would just vanish into another dimension. But do I actually have a specific memory of a teacher standing at the front of the room, calling my name, and me panicking because I realized I lost the homework? I just don't. \n\nIt’s actually kind of frustrating because I really want to give you a solid \"yes\" or \"no\" to help you piece this all together, but that whole era is just a giant gap in my memory. It’s like... elementary school, I clearly remember the desk moving. High school, I remember the physical exhaustion and the soccer. But those middle years? It's just a complete wash. I really wish I could give you a better answer, but I just don't remember them having to remind me, or me losing things back then. If it happened, it just completely faded out of my brain.\n\nDoctor: I understand middle school is fuzzy, but let me ask it this way: When you were in sixth or seventh grade, if you sat down to do homework, could you actually sit there and focus on it for, say, thirty minutes, or did you find yourself getting distracted and having to start over multiple times—even back then, before soccer became everything?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask about sitting down for thirty minutes, because I’m sitting here really trying to picture my actual bedroom from back then to see if it triggers anything. We lived in this older split-level house at the time, and my room was right down the short hallway from the living room. I had this little wooden desk—it was actually a hand-me-down from my older cousin, and it had these weird, clunky carved handles that would always get stuck when you tried to open the drawers—and I remember sitting there. I can picture the desk perfectly. I even remember this little metal desk lamp I had that would get ridiculously hot if you left it on for more than twenty minutes. You'd literally burn your hand if you bumped into it. \n\nBut if you’re asking me if I actually sat there and focused on a math worksheet for a solid half hour? Or if my mind was wandering and I kept having to start over? I... I honestly just couldn't tell you. It’s like I can picture the exact setting, the room, the desk, the hot lamp, but the actual act of doing the homework is just... it's just not there. I don't know if I stared at the wall for an hour, or if I somehow knocked it out in ten minutes, or if I was constantly getting up to go to the kitchen to get a snack. \n\nI feel like maybe I used to do it on the floor sometimes, too, instead of the desk? Like, maybe while the TV was on in the other room? I can sort of imagine hearing the local news or whatever my parents were watching echoing down the hall while I had my books spread out on the carpet. But trying to remember the specific feeling of being distracted or having to restart my work back in sixth or seventh grade... I just don't have it. I know exactly how my brain works right now, with the YouTube videos and all that, but back then? It’s just a complete blank. I really wish I could give you a clear yes or no on the thirty minutes thing, but I just can't pull up a single memory of how I actually functioned when I sat down to do it.\n\nDoctor: I know you can't remember every detail, but let me ask you this: Did your parents or teachers ever express concern about your grades or your ability to keep up academically in middle school, even before soccer became such a big part of your life?\n\nPatient: Man, you are really digging into the middle school vault today. Grades... okay, let me try to think about report cards back then. We used to get them mailed directly to the house at the end of every quarter, and they always came in those little white window envelopes. I remember my mom would usually be the one to open them because she always grabbed the mail from the box at the end of the driveway first thing. She would stand in the kitchen, usually right by the landline phone—we had this massive, ridiculously long curly cord on it that could stretch all the way across the room to the fridge—and she would open the envelope right there. \n\nBut as for her actually expressing concern, or my dad sitting me down because I wasn't keeping up? I just don't think so. Or at least, I have zero memory of it. I feel like if I was totally bombing out and failing my classes, I would definitely remember the fallout from that, right? Like, you remember getting grounded or losing your privileges. I had a PlayStation 2 back then, and I played it constantly, so if they had taken that away because of my grades, that would be burned into my brain. But I don't remember any big, dramatic interventions or them telling me I needed a tutor or anything like that. \n\nAnd with the teachers, it was kind of a different vibe than elementary school. That was the first time we had to switch rooms for every subject, so instead of one teacher who knew everything about you, you were just one of like thirty kids cycling through their room for forty-five minutes. So maybe I was just flying under the radar. Maybe I was just pulling solid C-minuses and blending in with the background, so nobody felt the need to sound the alarm. I really don't remember any teacher ever holding me after the bell rang to tell me they were worried about my academic progress. If they did, or if my parents were secretly stressing out about it behind closed doors, they kept it from me, because I just don't have any memory of anyone being concerned about it back then.\n\nDoctor: I want to make sure I understand the timeline correctly. You've told me that in elementary school—second, third grade—you were definitely fidgety, talking out of turn, and making careless mistakes on worksheets. But when you think back to that time, do you also remember your mind wandering or getting distracted from your schoolwork the way it does now with those job applications—where you lose an hour to something completely unrelated?\n\nPatient: Well, when you compare it to the job applications... I mean, the way it happens now is just so frustrating, because the main thing is I get pulled off track by every little sound or thought. It’s like I have zero filter for the rest of the world. I’ll be sitting there at my laptop, trying to work on an application—typing in my address, trying to remember the exact months I worked at my last job—and then a car will drive by outside. Not even a loud car, just tires on the wet pavement. Or my phone will vibrate on the desk, or, honestly, it doesn't even have to be a real sound. It could just be some random idea that pops into my head, like wondering if I left my wet towel on the bed or trying to remember the name of an actor from a movie I watched three weeks ago. And that just sends me somewhere else entirely before I even notice it's happening. I don't make a conscious choice to stop working on the application, I just suddenly snap back to reality and realize I've been watching YouTube videos about restoring old cast iron skillets for an hour. \n\nBut back in second or third grade? Did I do that? I mean, obviously I wasn't falling down internet rabbit holes, but... yeah, I think the actual feeling of it was exactly the same. \n\nI remember my third-grade classroom had those individual desks that the teacher would push together into little groups of four or five, you know, to make little pods? And if we were supposed to be doing a spelling worksheet, I wouldn't be doing it, because I'd hear the radiator clanking by the window. Or someone across the room would get up to use the pencil sharpener—one of those old, heavy metal crank ones bolted to the wall that made that awful grinding noise—and my brain would just instantly lock onto that sound. Then I'd be watching them sharpen the pencil, and then I'd look past them out the window at the playground, and I'd start thinking about recess, and I'd just be completely gone in my own head. \n\nSo I wasn't losing an hour to a phone, because I didn't have one, but I was definitely getting hijacked by every little distraction in that classroom before I even realized I wasn't doing my work. The teacher would say time was up, and I'd look down, and I had maybe written my name at the top of the paper. So yeah, the stuff distracting me has changed, but that immediate pull away from whatever I'm supposed to be focusing on? That was definitely happening back then, too."
